========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess what brought me in is… I’ve been getting really caught up in this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not nearly as noticeable as it seems to me, and part of me feels silly even saying it out loud. But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.

It’s like before I can even settle into a conversation, I’m wondering if they’ve noticed it, or if they’re thinking about it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I keep trying to tell myself people have better things to look at or think about, but it still feels so obvious to me. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.

Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still doing things, mostly, but it slows me down and makes me avoid certain things.

With my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I’ll get caught up before I leave the house or before I sit down to work, trying different sleeves or bracelets or positioning my wrist so it won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m already worked up, so it takes me longer to focus.

And socially… yes, I’ve skipped some choir things at church and a couple of volunteer commitments with the theater group because I just couldn’t get past the idea that people would notice my wrist. It sounds so small when I say it, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it or hide it before I can even show up.

Yes… it does feel hard to control, especially once I’m somewhere with other people. I don’t know if I’m counting the minutes exactly, but it can take over my mind in a way that feels bigger than it should.

Like at church, I’ll be sitting there trying to listen, and then I’ll suddenly think, “Can they see my wrist from this angle?” or “Did that person look down at it?” And then I’m not really present anymore. Same with bridge — I’ll be trying to focus on the cards, but I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to the scar, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.

And I do know, somewhere in my head, that it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I know people may not even be paying attention. But that doesn’t stop the feeling. Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.

Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re worried about the scar being noticeable, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking how it looks, covering it, or asking others if it’s okay—to try to feel more reassured?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before I leave the house, I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times, try different sleeves or a bracelet to cover it, and sometimes ask my husband if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. I wish I didn’t, because I can see how it probably sounds a little excessive, but before I leave the house I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror, usually more than once. I’ll look at it one way, then turn my arm, then think maybe the light is making it look worse, so I’ll check it again from another angle.

And then I’ll start fussing with what I’m wearing. I’ll try a blouse with longer sleeves, or I’ll put on a bracelet and see if it distracts from it or covers it enough. Sometimes I change back and forth a few times, which is embarrassing to admit, because it’s just my wrist, but it feels very hard to leave it alone once I’ve noticed it.

I do ask my husband sometimes, too. I’ll say, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it right away?” And he always tells me it’s barely there, that nobody would notice it. I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.

Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. I don’t think I’d say I’m convinced everyone is lying to me or anything like that, because part of me does know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of understand that, logically.

But when I’m actually out somewhere, like at church or when I’m playing bridge, it feels different. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m sure people’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite and not saying anything, or maybe they don’t realize how obvious it is because they’re not looking at it the way I am.

So… yes, I suppose I do feel like there’s something about it that I’m seeing more clearly than other people are, even though another part of me knows I may be making it larger in my mind than it really is. It’s confusing, because I don’t want to be vain or dramatic about it, but in the moment it feels very real.

I'm Dr. [Name]. Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brought me in is… I’ve been getting really caught up in this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not nearly as noticeable as it seems to me, and part of me feels silly even saying it out loud. But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like before I can even settle into a conversation, I’m wondering if they’ve noticed it, or if they’re thinking about it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I keep trying to tell myself people have better things to look at or think about, but it still feels so obvious to me. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your scar cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your ability to do things you normally would, like going to church or playing bridge?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still doing things, mostly, but it slows me down and makes me avoid certain things.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I’ll get caught up before I leave the house or before I sit down to work, trying different sleeves or bracelets or positioning my wrist so it won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m already worked up, so it takes me longer to focus.\n\nAnd socially… yes, I’ve skipped some choir things at church and a couple of volunteer commitments with the theater group because I just couldn’t get past the idea that people would notice my wrist. It sounds so small when I say it, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it or hide it before I can even show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar for a significant amount of time each day, maybe more than an hour, or does it feel hard to control those thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel hard to control, especially once I’m somewhere with other people. I don’t know if I’m counting the minutes exactly, but it can take over my mind in a way that feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be sitting there trying to listen, and then I’ll suddenly think, “Can they see my wrist from this angle?” or “Did that person look down at it?” And then I’m not really present anymore. Same with bridge — I’ll be trying to focus on the cards, but I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to the scar, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I do know, somewhere in my head, that it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I know people may not even be paying attention. But that doesn’t stop the feeling. Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about the scar being noticeable, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking how it looks, covering it, or asking others if it’s okay—to try to feel more reassured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I wish I didn’t, because I can see how it probably sounds a little excessive, but before I leave the house I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror, usually more than once. I’ll look at it one way, then turn my arm, then think maybe the light is making it look worse, so I’ll check it again from another angle.\n\nAnd then I’ll start fussing with what I’m wearing. I’ll try a blouse with longer sleeves, or I’ll put on a bracelet and see if it distracts from it or covers it enough. Sometimes I change back and forth a few times, which is embarrassing to admit, because it’s just my wrist, but it feels very hard to leave it alone once I’ve noticed it.\n\nI do ask my husband sometimes, too. I’ll say, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it right away?” And he always tells me it’s barely there, that nobody would notice it. I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you ever feel like it’s not just noticeable but somehow worse than others seem to realize—like there’s something about it that others don’t understand or aren’t seeing the way you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. I don’t think I’d say I’m convinced everyone is lying to me or anything like that, because part of me does know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of understand that, logically.\n\nBut when I’m actually out somewhere, like at church or when I’m playing bridge, it feels different. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m sure people’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite and not saying anything, or maybe they don’t realize how obvious it is because they’re not looking at it the way I am.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I do feel like there’s something about it that I’m seeing more clearly than other people are, even though another part of me knows I may be making it larger in my mind than it really is. It’s confusing, because I don’t want to be vain or dramatic about it, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more about how it looks… how it looks to me, anyway. It’s not just a random thought that pops in and bothers me no matter what. It’s really tied to the scar being there on my wrist and feeling like it looks unattractive, or obvious, or like there’s something flawed about it that other people either don’t notice or don’t admit they notice.

I do know, in a more reasonable part of my mind, that it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it doesn’t feel small at all. I’ll be trying to listen or pay attention to the game, and I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. And then I feel so self-conscious, like I want to turn my arm or hide it without making it obvious that I’m hiding it.

So yes, I’d say it’s mainly about the appearance of it — the idea that it looks flawed or noticeable in a way that maybe other people don’t see the same way I do.

But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like before I can even settle into a conversation, I’m wondering if they’ve noticed it, or if they’re thinking about it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I keep trying to tell myself people have better things to look at or think about, but it still feels so obvious to me. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your scar cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your ability to do things you normally would, like going to church or playing bridge?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still doing things, mostly, but it slows me down and makes me avoid certain things.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I’ll get caught up before I leave the house or before I sit down to work, trying different sleeves or bracelets or positioning my wrist so it won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m already worked up, so it takes me longer to focus.\n\nAnd socially… yes, I’ve skipped some choir things at church and a couple of volunteer commitments with the theater group because I just couldn’t get past the idea that people would notice my wrist. It sounds so small when I say it, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it or hide it before I can even show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar for a significant amount of time each day, maybe more than an hour, or does it feel hard to control those thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel hard to control, especially once I’m somewhere with other people. I don’t know if I’m counting the minutes exactly, but it can take over my mind in a way that feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be sitting there trying to listen, and then I’ll suddenly think, “Can they see my wrist from this angle?” or “Did that person look down at it?” And then I’m not really present anymore. Same with bridge — I’ll be trying to focus on the cards, but I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to the scar, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I do know, somewhere in my head, that it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I know people may not even be paying attention. But that doesn’t stop the feeling. Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about the scar being noticeable, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking how it looks, covering it, or asking others if it’s okay—to try to feel more reassured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I wish I didn’t, because I can see how it probably sounds a little excessive, but before I leave the house I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror, usually more than once. I’ll look at it one way, then turn my arm, then think maybe the light is making it look worse, so I’ll check it again from another angle.\n\nAnd then I’ll start fussing with what I’m wearing. I’ll try a blouse with longer sleeves, or I’ll put on a bracelet and see if it distracts from it or covers it enough. Sometimes I change back and forth a few times, which is embarrassing to admit, because it’s just my wrist, but it feels very hard to leave it alone once I’ve noticed it.\n\nI do ask my husband sometimes, too. I’ll say, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it right away?” And he always tells me it’s barely there, that nobody would notice it. I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you ever feel like it’s not just noticeable but somehow worse than others seem to realize—like there’s something about it that others don’t understand or aren’t seeing the way you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. I don’t think I’d say I’m convinced everyone is lying to me or anything like that, because part of me does know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of understand that, logically.\n\nBut when I’m actually out somewhere, like at church or when I’m playing bridge, it feels different. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m sure people’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite and not saying anything, or maybe they don’t realize how obvious it is because they’re not looking at it the way I am.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I do feel like there’s something about it that I’m seeing more clearly than other people are, even though another part of me knows I may be making it larger in my mind than it really is. It’s confusing, because I don’t want to be vain or dramatic about it, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're focused on the scar, is it mainly the idea that it looks physically unattractive or flawed in a way that others don't seem to see, or is it more about intrusive, upsetting thoughts that keep coming back regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how it looks… how it looks to me, anyway. It’s not just a random thought that pops in and bothers me no matter what. It’s really tied to the scar being there on my wrist and feeling like it looks unattractive, or obvious, or like there’s something flawed about it that other people either don’t notice or don’t admit they notice.\n\nI do know, in a more reasonable part of my mind, that it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it doesn’t feel small at all. I’ll be trying to listen or pay attention to the game, and I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. And then I feel so self-conscious, like I want to turn my arm or hide it without making it obvious that I’m hiding it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mainly about the appearance of it — the idea that it looks flawed or noticeable in a way that maybe other people don’t see the same way I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder (F42.8) remains plausible due to intrusive thoughts and repetitive behaviors, but the primary focus on appearance makes F45.2 more likely. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mainly the scar itself. I mean, I can have the usual complaints anyone has at my age, I suppose, but nothing that takes hold of me the way this does.

With the wrist, it’s like once I notice it, I can’t quite un-notice it. I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge, I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it — like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I get caught up in thinking about how my arm is positioned, whether the light is hitting it, whether I should turn my wrist a little.

So no, it’s not really other parts of my body in the same way. It’s this scar. It’s small, probably, to other people, but to me it starts feeling very obvious.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brought me in is… I’ve been getting really caught up in this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not nearly as noticeable as it seems to me, and part of me feels silly even saying it out loud. But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like before I can even settle into a conversation, I’m wondering if they’ve noticed it, or if they’re thinking about it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I keep trying to tell myself people have better things to look at or think about, but it still feels so obvious to me. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your scar cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your ability to do things you normally would, like going to church or playing bridge?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still doing things, mostly, but it slows me down and makes me avoid certain things.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I’ll get caught up before I leave the house or before I sit down to work, trying different sleeves or bracelets or positioning my wrist so it won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m already worked up, so it takes me longer to focus.\n\nAnd socially… yes, I’ve skipped some choir things at church and a couple of volunteer commitments with the theater group because I just couldn’t get past the idea that people would notice my wrist. It sounds so small when I say it, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it or hide it before I can even show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar for a significant amount of time each day, maybe more than an hour, or does it feel hard to control those thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel hard to control, especially once I’m somewhere with other people. I don’t know if I’m counting the minutes exactly, but it can take over my mind in a way that feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be sitting there trying to listen, and then I’ll suddenly think, “Can they see my wrist from this angle?” or “Did that person look down at it?” And then I’m not really present anymore. Same with bridge — I’ll be trying to focus on the cards, but I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to the scar, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I do know, somewhere in my head, that it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I know people may not even be paying attention. But that doesn’t stop the feeling. Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about the scar being noticeable, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking how it looks, covering it, or asking others if it’s okay—to try to feel more reassured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I wish I didn’t, because I can see how it probably sounds a little excessive, but before I leave the house I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror, usually more than once. I’ll look at it one way, then turn my arm, then think maybe the light is making it look worse, so I’ll check it again from another angle.\n\nAnd then I’ll start fussing with what I’m wearing. I’ll try a blouse with longer sleeves, or I’ll put on a bracelet and see if it distracts from it or covers it enough. Sometimes I change back and forth a few times, which is embarrassing to admit, because it’s just my wrist, but it feels very hard to leave it alone once I’ve noticed it.\n\nI do ask my husband sometimes, too. I’ll say, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it right away?” And he always tells me it’s barely there, that nobody would notice it. I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you ever feel like it’s not just noticeable but somehow worse than others seem to realize—like there’s something about it that others don’t understand or aren’t seeing the way you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. I don’t think I’d say I’m convinced everyone is lying to me or anything like that, because part of me does know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of understand that, logically.\n\nBut when I’m actually out somewhere, like at church or when I’m playing bridge, it feels different. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m sure people’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite and not saying anything, or maybe they don’t realize how obvious it is because they’re not looking at it the way I am.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I do feel like there’s something about it that I’m seeing more clearly than other people are, even though another part of me knows I may be making it larger in my mind than it really is. It’s confusing, because I don’t want to be vain or dramatic about it, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're focused on the scar, is it mainly the idea that it looks physically unattractive or flawed in a way that others don't seem to see, or is it more about intrusive, upsetting thoughts that keep coming back regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how it looks… how it looks to me, anyway. It’s not just a random thought that pops in and bothers me no matter what. It’s really tied to the scar being there on my wrist and feeling like it looks unattractive, or obvious, or like there’s something flawed about it that other people either don’t notice or don’t admit they notice.\n\nI do know, in a more reasonable part of my mind, that it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it doesn’t feel small at all. I’ll be trying to listen or pay attention to the game, and I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. And then I feel so self-conscious, like I want to turn my arm or hide it without making it obvious that I’m hiding it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mainly about the appearance of it — the idea that it looks flawed or noticeable in a way that maybe other people don’t see the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about other parts of your body in the same way you worry about your wrist scar, or is this concern mainly limited to the scar itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the scar itself. I mean, I can have the usual complaints anyone has at my age, I suppose, but nothing that takes hold of me the way this does.\n\nWith the wrist, it’s like once I notice it, I can’t quite un-notice it. I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that. It’s small, probably, to other people, but to me it starts feeling very obvious.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they will judge you negatively as a person because of it, or is your main concern that the scar itself looks worse than others seem to realize?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but when I’m at church or bridge I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more the second one… that the scar itself looks worse than other people seem to realize. I don’t exactly think they’re judging my whole character or thinking I’m a bad person because of it. It’s not quite that.

It’s more that I feel like they must be seeing this mark on my wrist, and maybe they’re just being kind or polite by not saying anything. I know, sitting here talking to you, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m at bridge and my hand is out on the table, it just feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.

And then I start thinking, maybe they don’t understand how obvious it is, or maybe they’re seeing it but pretending not to. So no, I wouldn’t say my main fear is that they’ll judge me as a person. It’s more that the scar feels so noticeable and unattractive to me, and I can’t quite believe other people don’t see it that way too.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brought me in is… I’ve been getting really caught up in this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not nearly as noticeable as it seems to me, and part of me feels silly even saying it out loud. But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like before I can even settle into a conversation, I’m wondering if they’ve noticed it, or if they’re thinking about it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I keep trying to tell myself people have better things to look at or think about, but it still feels so obvious to me. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your scar cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your ability to do things you normally would, like going to church or playing bridge?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still doing things, mostly, but it slows me down and makes me avoid certain things.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I’ll get caught up before I leave the house or before I sit down to work, trying different sleeves or bracelets or positioning my wrist so it won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m already worked up, so it takes me longer to focus.\n\nAnd socially… yes, I’ve skipped some choir things at church and a couple of volunteer commitments with the theater group because I just couldn’t get past the idea that people would notice my wrist. It sounds so small when I say it, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it or hide it before I can even show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar for a significant amount of time each day, maybe more than an hour, or does it feel hard to control those thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel hard to control, especially once I’m somewhere with other people. I don’t know if I’m counting the minutes exactly, but it can take over my mind in a way that feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be sitting there trying to listen, and then I’ll suddenly think, “Can they see my wrist from this angle?” or “Did that person look down at it?” And then I’m not really present anymore. Same with bridge — I’ll be trying to focus on the cards, but I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to the scar, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I do know, somewhere in my head, that it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I know people may not even be paying attention. But that doesn’t stop the feeling. Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about the scar being noticeable, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking how it looks, covering it, or asking others if it’s okay—to try to feel more reassured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I wish I didn’t, because I can see how it probably sounds a little excessive, but before I leave the house I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror, usually more than once. I’ll look at it one way, then turn my arm, then think maybe the light is making it look worse, so I’ll check it again from another angle.\n\nAnd then I’ll start fussing with what I’m wearing. I’ll try a blouse with longer sleeves, or I’ll put on a bracelet and see if it distracts from it or covers it enough. Sometimes I change back and forth a few times, which is embarrassing to admit, because it’s just my wrist, but it feels very hard to leave it alone once I’ve noticed it.\n\nI do ask my husband sometimes, too. I’ll say, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it right away?” And he always tells me it’s barely there, that nobody would notice it. I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you ever feel like it’s not just noticeable but somehow worse than others seem to realize—like there’s something about it that others don’t understand or aren’t seeing the way you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. I don’t think I’d say I’m convinced everyone is lying to me or anything like that, because part of me does know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of understand that, logically.\n\nBut when I’m actually out somewhere, like at church or when I’m playing bridge, it feels different. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m sure people’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite and not saying anything, or maybe they don’t realize how obvious it is because they’re not looking at it the way I am.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I do feel like there’s something about it that I’m seeing more clearly than other people are, even though another part of me knows I may be making it larger in my mind than it really is. It’s confusing, because I don’t want to be vain or dramatic about it, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're focused on the scar, is it mainly the idea that it looks physically unattractive or flawed in a way that others don't seem to see, or is it more about intrusive, upsetting thoughts that keep coming back regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how it looks… how it looks to me, anyway. It’s not just a random thought that pops in and bothers me no matter what. It’s really tied to the scar being there on my wrist and feeling like it looks unattractive, or obvious, or like there’s something flawed about it that other people either don’t notice or don’t admit they notice.\n\nI do know, in a more reasonable part of my mind, that it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it doesn’t feel small at all. I’ll be trying to listen or pay attention to the game, and I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. And then I feel so self-conscious, like I want to turn my arm or hide it without making it obvious that I’m hiding it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mainly about the appearance of it — the idea that it looks flawed or noticeable in a way that maybe other people don’t see the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about other parts of your body in the same way you worry about your wrist scar, or is this concern mainly limited to the scar itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the scar itself. I mean, I can have the usual complaints anyone has at my age, I suppose, but nothing that takes hold of me the way this does.\n\nWith the wrist, it’s like once I notice it, I can’t quite un-notice it. I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge, I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it — like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I get caught up in thinking about how my arm is positioned, whether the light is hitting it, whether I should turn my wrist a little.\n\nSo no, it’s not really other parts of my body in the same way. It’s this scar. It’s small, probably, to other people, but to me it starts feeling very obvious.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they will judge you negatively as a person because of it, or is your main concern that the scar itself looks worse than others seem to realize?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one… that the scar itself looks worse than other people seem to realize. I don’t exactly think they’re judging my whole character or thinking I’m a bad person because of it. It’s not quite that.\n\nIt’s more that I feel like they must be seeing this mark on my wrist, and maybe they’re just being kind or polite by not saying anything. I know, sitting here talking to you, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m at bridge and my hand is out on the table, it just feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, maybe they don’t understand how obvious it is, or maybe they’re seeing it but pretending not to. So no, I wouldn’t say my main fear is that they’ll judge me as a person. It’s more that the scar feels so noticeable and unattractive to me, and I can’t quite believe other people don’t see it that way too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it sounds so small when I put it into words. But it has interfered.

With my bookkeeping work, there have been times I’ve been late turning things in because I get stuck before I leave the house, trying to decide what to wear or how to position my sleeve so my wrist won’t show. Or I’ll sit down to work and I’m supposed to be concentrating on figures, and instead I’m looking at my wrist or thinking about whether someone noticed it earlier, and then I lose time. It’s not that I can’t do the work, exactly, but I’ve turned things in later than I should have because I got caught up in all of that.

And I’ve skipped some things too. I sing in the choir, and I help with theater volunteer work sometimes, and there have been days where I just… didn’t go. I told myself I was tired or that they had enough people, but really it was because I kept imagining standing there under lights or sitting where people could see my arm, and I couldn’t stop thinking about whether the scar would be noticeable.

So yes, it’s not ruining everything, but it has caused problems. More than I’d like to admit.

But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to it.\n\nIt’s like before I can even settle into a conversation, I’m wondering if they’ve noticed it, or if they’re thinking about it, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. I keep trying to tell myself people have better things to look at or think about, but it still feels so obvious to me. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your scar cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your ability to do things you normally would, like going to church or playing bridge?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still doing things, mostly, but it slows me down and makes me avoid certain things.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I’ll get caught up before I leave the house or before I sit down to work, trying different sleeves or bracelets or positioning my wrist so it won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m already worked up, so it takes me longer to focus.\n\nAnd socially… yes, I’ve skipped some choir things at church and a couple of volunteer commitments with the theater group because I just couldn’t get past the idea that people would notice my wrist. It sounds so small when I say it, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it or hide it before I can even show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar for a significant amount of time each day, maybe more than an hour, or does it feel hard to control those thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel hard to control, especially once I’m somewhere with other people. I don’t know if I’m counting the minutes exactly, but it can take over my mind in a way that feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be sitting there trying to listen, and then I’ll suddenly think, “Can they see my wrist from this angle?” or “Did that person look down at it?” And then I’m not really present anymore. Same with bridge — I’ll be trying to focus on the cards, but I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to the scar, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I do know, somewhere in my head, that it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I know people may not even be paying attention. But that doesn’t stop the feeling. Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about the scar being noticeable, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking how it looks, covering it, or asking others if it’s okay—to try to feel more reassured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I wish I didn’t, because I can see how it probably sounds a little excessive, but before I leave the house I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror, usually more than once. I’ll look at it one way, then turn my arm, then think maybe the light is making it look worse, so I’ll check it again from another angle.\n\nAnd then I’ll start fussing with what I’m wearing. I’ll try a blouse with longer sleeves, or I’ll put on a bracelet and see if it distracts from it or covers it enough. I’ll say, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it right away?” And he always tells me it’s barely there, that nobody would notice it. I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you ever feel like it’s not just noticeable but somehow worse than others seem to realize—like there’s something about it that others don’t understand or aren’t seeing the way you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think that’s really where my mind goes. I’m not sitting there thinking it’s a disease, exactly, or that it means something is wrong inside me. It’s more… I don’t like the way it looks, and I get stuck on that.

I suppose if I’m being very precise, I worry about what it looks like on the surface — the color of it, the way the skin catches the light, whether it seems raised or obvious. But I’m not usually thinking, “Oh, this is cancer,” or “this means I’m ill.” It isn’t that kind of fear.

It’s more vain than that, I guess, though I hate saying it that way. It just feels ugly to me, and then I can’t stop noticing it.

It’s been bothering me enough that I thought maybe I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Does this concern about your scar cause you a lot of distress, or has it affected your ability to do things you normally would, like going to church or playing bridge?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. I mean, I’m still doing things, mostly, but it slows me down and makes me avoid certain things.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I’ll get caught up before I leave the house or before I sit down to work, trying different sleeves or bracelets or positioning my wrist so it won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m already worked up, so it takes me longer to focus.\n\nAnd socially… yes, I’ve skipped some choir things at church and a couple of volunteer commitments with the theater group because I just couldn’t get past the idea that people would notice my wrist. It sounds so small when I say it, but in the moment it feels like I have to fix it or hide it before I can even show up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about the scar for a significant amount of time each day, maybe more than an hour, or does it feel hard to control those thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel hard to control, especially once I’m somewhere with other people. I don’t know if I’m counting the minutes exactly, but it can take over my mind in a way that feels bigger than it should.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be sitting there trying to listen, and then I’ll suddenly think, “Can they see my wrist from this angle?” or “Did that person look down at it?” And then I’m not really present anymore. Same with bridge — I’ll be trying to focus on the cards, but I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to the scar, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd I do know, somewhere in my head, that it probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I know people may not even be paying attention. But that doesn’t stop the feeling. Once the thought is there, it just sort of keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about the scar being noticeable, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking how it looks, covering it, or asking others if it’s okay—to try to feel more reassured?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I wish I didn’t, because I can see how it probably sounds a little excessive, but before I leave the house I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror, usually more than once. I’ll look at it one way, then turn my arm, then think maybe the light is making it look worse, so I’ll check it again from another angle.\n\nAnd then I’ll start fussing with what I’m wearing. I’ll try a blouse with longer sleeves, or I’ll put on a bracelet and see if it distracts from it or covers it enough. Sometimes I change back and forth a few times, which is embarrassing to admit, because it’s just my wrist, but it feels very hard to leave it alone once I’ve noticed it.\n\nI do ask my husband sometimes, too. I’ll say, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it right away?” And he always tells me it’s barely there, that nobody would notice it. I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you ever feel like it’s not just noticeable but somehow worse than others seem to realize—like there’s something about it that others don’t understand or aren’t seeing the way you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. I don’t think I’d say I’m convinced everyone is lying to me or anything like that, because part of me does know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of understand that, logically.\n\nBut when I’m actually out somewhere, like at church or when I’m playing bridge, it feels different. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m sure people’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite and not saying anything, or maybe they don’t realize how obvious it is because they’re not looking at it the way I am.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I do feel like there’s something about it that I’m seeing more clearly than other people are, even though another part of me knows I may be making it larger in my mind than it really is. It’s confusing, because I don’t want to be vain or dramatic about it, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're focused on the scar, is it mainly the idea that it looks physically unattractive or flawed in a way that others don't seem to see, or is it more about intrusive, upsetting thoughts that keep coming back regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how it looks… how it looks to me, anyway. It’s not just a random thought that pops in and bothers me no matter what. It’s really tied to the scar being there on my wrist and feeling like it looks unattractive, or obvious, or like there’s something flawed about it that other people either don’t notice or don’t admit they notice.\n\nI do know, in a more reasonable part of my mind, that it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it doesn’t feel small at all. I’ll be trying to listen or pay attention to the game, and I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. And then I feel so self-conscious, like I want to turn my arm or hide it without making it obvious that I’m hiding it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mainly about the appearance of it — the idea that it looks flawed or noticeable in a way that maybe other people don’t see the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about other parts of your body in the same way you worry about your wrist scar, or is this concern mainly limited to the scar itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the scar itself. I mean, I can have the usual complaints anyone has at my age, I suppose, but nothing that takes hold of me the way this does.\n\nWith the wrist, it’s like once I notice it, I can’t quite un-notice it. I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge, I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it — like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I get caught up in thinking about how my arm is positioned, whether the light is hitting it, whether I should turn my wrist a little.\n\nSo no, it’s not really other parts of my body in the same way. It’s this scar. It’s small, probably, to other people, but to me it starts feeling very obvious.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they will judge you negatively as a person because of it, or is your main concern that the scar itself looks worse than others seem to realize?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one… that the scar itself looks worse than other people seem to realize. I don’t exactly think they’re judging my whole character or thinking I’m a bad person because of it. It’s not quite that.\n\nIt’s more that I feel like they must be seeing this mark on my wrist, and maybe they’re just being kind or polite by not saying anything. I know, sitting here talking to you, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m at bridge and my hand is out on the table, it just feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, maybe they don’t understand how obvious it is, or maybe they’re seeing it but pretending not to. So no, I wouldn’t say my main fear is that they’ll judge me as a person. It’s more that the scar feels so noticeable and unattractive to me, and I can’t quite believe other people don’t see it that way too.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your wrist scar ever led to problems in your relationships, work, or other important areas of your life that we haven't talked about yet?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it sounds so small when I put it into words. But it has interfered.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, there have been times I’ve been late turning things in because I get stuck before I leave the house, trying to decide what to wear or how to position my sleeve so my wrist won’t show. Or I’ll sit down to work and I’m supposed to be concentrating on figures, and instead I’m looking at my wrist or thinking about whether someone noticed it earlier, and then I lose time. It’s not that I can’t do the work, exactly, but I’ve turned things in later than I should have because I got caught up in all of that.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped some things too. I sing in the choir, and I help with theater volunteer work sometimes, and there have been days where I just… didn’t go. I told myself I was tired or that they had enough people, but really it was because I kept imagining standing there under lights or sitting where people could see my arm, and I couldn’t stop thinking about whether the scar would be noticeable.\n\nSo yes, it’s not ruining everything, but it has caused problems. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, are you ever worried that it might be a sign of something more serious, like a disease or a deeper health problem?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s really where my mind goes. I’m not sitting there thinking it’s a disease, exactly, or that it means something is wrong inside me. It’s more… I don’t like the way it looks, and I get stuck on that.\n\nI suppose if I’m being very precise, I worry about what it looks like on the surface — the color of it, the way the skin catches the light, whether it seems raised or obvious. But I’m not usually thinking, “Oh, this is cancer,” or “this means I’m ill.” It isn’t that kind of fear.\n\nIt’s more vain than that, I guess, though I hate saying it that way. It just feels ugly to me, and then I can’t stop noticing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. It’s not only covering it, though that’s part of it. It’s more that I start arranging whole little pieces of my day around whether my wrist might be seen, and then it gets away from me.

Like with my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I get caught up before I even leave the house, fussing with what I’m wearing or thinking through whether I’ll have to reach across a desk or hand someone papers. And then sometimes I lose time just trying to make sure my wrist won’t be noticed, or I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not focusing the way I should.

And I’ve skipped things I actually care about, too. Choir, for example, and some of the theater volunteer commitments. I’ll tell myself, “Oh, they have enough people,” or “I’m too tired tonight,” but if I’m honest, a lot of it is that I imagine being there and someone seeing my wrist, especially if my arm is out or there are lights or people close by. Then I talk myself out of going.

So yes, I guess I do change my behavior. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds excessive, but I have avoided things because of how the scar looks.

I believe that he’s trying to be kind, and maybe he’s right, but it only settles me for a little while. Then I’ll start wondering if he’s just saying that because he’s my husband.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, do you ever feel like it’s not just noticeable but somehow worse than others seem to realize—like there’s something about it that others don’t understand or aren’t seeing the way you do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. I don’t think I’d say I’m convinced everyone is lying to me or anything like that, because part of me does know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of understand that, logically.\n\nBut when I’m actually out somewhere, like at church or when I’m playing bridge, it feels different. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m sure people’s eyes are going right to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I think, well, maybe they’re just being polite and not saying anything, or maybe they don’t realize how obvious it is because they’re not looking at it the way I am.\n\nSo… yes, I suppose I do feel like there’s something about it that I’m seeing more clearly than other people are, even though another part of me knows I may be making it larger in my mind than it really is. It’s confusing, because I don’t want to be vain or dramatic about it, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're focused on the scar, is it mainly the idea that it looks physically unattractive or flawed in a way that others don't seem to see, or is it more about intrusive, upsetting thoughts that keep coming back regardless of how it looks?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more about how it looks… how it looks to me, anyway. It’s not just a random thought that pops in and bothers me no matter what. It’s really tied to the scar being there on my wrist and feeling like it looks unattractive, or obvious, or like there’s something flawed about it that other people either don’t notice or don’t admit they notice.\n\nI do know, in a more reasonable part of my mind, that it’s probably not as bad as it seems to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it doesn’t feel small at all. I’ll be trying to listen or pay attention to the game, and I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist, like it’s the first thing they see about me now. And then I feel so self-conscious, like I want to turn my arm or hide it without making it obvious that I’m hiding it.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s mainly about the appearance of it — the idea that it looks flawed or noticeable in a way that maybe other people don’t see the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about other parts of your body in the same way you worry about your wrist scar, or is this concern mainly limited to the scar itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly the scar itself. I mean, I can have the usual complaints anyone has at my age, I suppose, but nothing that takes hold of me the way this does.\n\nWith the wrist, it’s like once I notice it, I can’t quite un-notice it. I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge, I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it — like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And then I get caught up in thinking about how my arm is positioned, whether the light is hitting it, whether I should turn my wrist a little.\n\nSo no, it’s not really other parts of my body in the same way. It’s this scar. It’s small, probably, to other people, but to me it starts feeling very obvious.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing your scar, is your main concern that they will judge you negatively as a person because of it, or is your main concern that the scar itself looks worse than others seem to realize?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the second one… that the scar itself looks worse than other people seem to realize. I don’t exactly think they’re judging my whole character or thinking I’m a bad person because of it. It’s not quite that.\n\nIt’s more that I feel like they must be seeing this mark on my wrist, and maybe they’re just being kind or polite by not saying anything. I know, sitting here talking to you, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m at bridge and my hand is out on the table, it just feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, maybe they don’t understand how obvious it is, or maybe they’re seeing it but pretending not to. So no, I wouldn’t say my main fear is that they’ll judge me as a person. It’s more that the scar feels so noticeable and unattractive to me, and I can’t quite believe other people don’t see it that way too.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your wrist scar ever led to problems in your relationships, work, or other important areas of your life that we haven't talked about yet?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it sounds so small when I put it into words. But it has interfered.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, there have been times I’ve been late turning things in because I get stuck before I leave the house, trying to decide what to wear or how to position my sleeve so my wrist won’t show. Or I’ll sit down to work and I’m supposed to be concentrating on figures, and instead I’m looking at my wrist or thinking about whether someone noticed it earlier, and then I lose time. It’s not that I can’t do the work, exactly, but I’ve turned things in later than I should have because I got caught up in all of that.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped some things too. I sing in the choir, and I help with theater volunteer work sometimes, and there have been days where I just… didn’t go. I told myself I was tired or that they had enough people, but really it was because I kept imagining standing there under lights or sitting where people could see my arm, and I couldn’t stop thinking about whether the scar would be noticeable.\n\nSo yes, it’s not ruining everything, but it has caused problems. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, are you ever worried that it might be a sign of something more serious, like a disease or a deeper health problem?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s really where my mind goes. I’m not sitting there thinking it’s a disease, exactly, or that it means something is wrong inside me. It’s more… I don’t like the way it looks, and I get stuck on that.\n\nI suppose if I’m being very precise, I worry about what it looks like on the surface — the color of it, the way the skin catches the light, whether it seems raised or obvious. But I’m not usually thinking, “Oh, this is cancer,” or “this means I’m ill.” It isn’t that kind of fear.\n\nIt’s more vain than that, I guess, though I hate saying it that way. It just feels ugly to me, and then I can’t stop noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or changing your behavior specifically because of how the scar looks, beyond just trying to cover it up?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not only covering it, though that’s part of it. It’s more that I start arranging whole little pieces of my day around whether my wrist might be seen, and then it gets away from me.\n\nLike with my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I get caught up before I even leave the house, fussing with what I’m wearing or thinking through whether I’ll have to reach across a desk or hand someone papers. And then sometimes I lose time just trying to make sure my wrist won’t be noticed, or I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not focusing the way I should.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped things I actually care about, too. Choir, for example, and some of the theater volunteer commitments. I’ll tell myself, “Oh, they have enough people,” or “I’m too tired tonight,” but if I’m honest, a lot of it is that I imagine being there and someone seeing my wrist, especially if my arm is out or there are lights or people close by. Then I talk myself out of going.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do change my behavior. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds excessive, but I have avoided things because of how the scar looks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has this concern about your wrist scar ever led to problems in your relationships, work, or other important areas of your life that we haven't talked about yet?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, though I feel a little embarrassed saying that because it sounds so small when I put it into words. Or I’ll sit down to work and I’m supposed to be concentrating on figures, and instead I’m looking at my wrist or thinking about whether someone noticed it earlier, and then I lose time. It’s not that I can’t do the work, exactly, but I’ve turned things in later than I should have because I got caught up in all of that.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped some things too. I sing in the choir, and I help with theater volunteer work sometimes, and there have been days where I just… didn’t go. I told myself I was tired or that they had enough people, but really it was because I kept imagining standing there under lights or sitting where people could see my arm, and I couldn’t stop thinking about whether the scar would be noticeable.\n\nSo yes, it’s not ruining everything, but it has caused problems. More than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the scar, are you ever worried that it might be a sign of something more serious, like a disease or a deeper health problem?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s really where my mind goes. I’m not sitting there thinking it’s a disease, exactly, or that it means something is wrong inside me. It’s more… I don’t like the way it looks, and I get stuck on that.\n\nI suppose if I’m being very precise, I worry about what it looks like on the surface — the color of it, the way the skin catches the light, whether it seems raised or obvious. But I’m not usually thinking, “Oh, this is cancer,” or “this means I’m ill.” It isn’t that kind of fear.\n\nIt’s more vain than that, I guess, though I hate saying it that way. It just feels ugly to me, and then I can’t stop noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or changing your behavior specifically because of how the scar looks, beyond just trying to cover it up?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s not only covering it, though that’s part of it. It’s more that I start arranging whole little pieces of my day around whether my wrist might be seen, and then it gets away from me.\n\nLike with my bookkeeping work, I’ve been late turning things in because I get caught up before I even leave the house, fussing with what I’m wearing or thinking through whether I’ll have to reach across a desk or hand someone papers. And then sometimes I lose time just trying to make sure my wrist won’t be noticed, or I get so preoccupied with it that I’m not focusing the way I should.\n\nAnd I’ve skipped things I actually care about, too. Choir, for example, and some of the theater volunteer commitments. I’ll tell myself, “Oh, they have enough people,” or “I’m too tired tonight,” but if I’m honest, a lot of it is that I imagine being there and someone seeing my wrist, especially if my arm is out or there are lights or people close by. Then I talk myself out of going.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do change my behavior. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds excessive, but I have avoided things because of how the scar looks.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever spend a lot of time grooming or adjusting your appearance in front of mirrors or other reflective surfaces because of the way the scar looks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before I leave the house, I’ll check my wrist in the hall mirror a few times, try different sleeves or a bracelet to cover it, and sometimes ask my husband if it looks obvious, even though he always says it’s barely there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. More than I’d like to admit, honestly.

Before I leave the house, especially if I’m going somewhere where I’ll be around people, I’ll stop at the hall mirror and check my wrist. Not just once, usually. I’ll look, then step away, then come back and look again, because I’ll think maybe the light was different or maybe I didn’t really see how noticeable it was. I’ll turn my arm a little, pull my sleeve down, push it back up to see if it slips, that sort of thing.

Sometimes I’ll change sleeves more than once — like I’ll think one blouse covers it, but then if I reach for something, the cuff moves and I can see it, so then I try another top. Or I’ll put on a bracelet and fuss with where it sits, whether it covers the scar or draws more attention to it. It can get a bit ridiculous, standing there adjusting something so small.

And I do ask my husband sometimes, “Does this look obvious?” or “Can you see it?” He always says it’s barely there, and I know he means to reassure me, but I don’t always believe it. Or I believe him for maybe a minute, and then I look again anyway.
